=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two length-N strings A = A_1A_2 \ldots A_N and B = B_1B_2 \ldots B_N, each consisting of 0 and 1.
There are N squares aligned in a row from left to right. For i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, the i-th square from the left is called square i. Initially, square i contains a piece if A_i =  1, and no piece if A_i =  0.
You may repeat the following operation any number of times (possibly zero):

- Choose an integer i between 1 and N, inclusive.
- Move all pieces simultaneously one square closer to square i. That is, for each piece, let square j be its current position and square j' be its new position, and the following holds:
- if i < j, then j' = j-1;
- if i > j, then j' = j+1;
- if i = j, then j' = j.



Determine whether it is possible to reach a configuration satisfying the following condition, and if it is possible, find the minimum number of operations needed to do so:

For every i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, there is at least one piece in square i if and only if B_i =  1.

You are given T independent test cases. Print the answer for each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Here, \mathrm{case}_i (i=1,2,\ldots,T) denotes the i-th test case.
Each test case is given in the following format:
N
A
B

Output

Print T lines.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, T, on the i-th line, print -1 if it is impossible to reach a configuration satisfying the condition for the i-th test case. Otherwise, print the minimum number of operations needed.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq N \leq 10^6
- T and N are integers.
- A and B are strings of length N, each consisting of 0 and 1.
- There exists i such that A_i =  1.
- There exists i such that B_i =  1.
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 10^6.

Sample Input 1

3
8
01001101
00001011
3
010
111
20
10100011011110101011
00010001111101100000

Sample Output 1

3
-1
5

The input has three independent test cases.
In the first test case, initially, the sequence of the numbers of pieces in the squares is (0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1). By performing the operation three times as follows, you can satisfy the condition:

- Choose i = 5. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose i = 8. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 1).
- Choose i = 8. After the operation, the configuration is (0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 2, 1).

It is impossible to satisfy the condition in fewer than three operations, so the answer is 3.
In the second test case, no matter how you perform the operations, you cannot satisfy the condition, so the answer is -1.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

- Choose i = p_r (occupied): left gap d_{r-1} decreases by1 (if exists), right gap d_r decreases by1 (if exists). Actually if i=p_r, p_r stays, p_{r-1}->p_{r-1}+1, p_{r+1}->p_{r+1}-1. So both adjacent gaps shrink by1. If no left/right, only one side.
- Choose i outside [p1,pm]: shift all by ±1, gaps unchanged. This changes absolute positions but not relative configuration. Since target positions matter, shifting can adjust.

Thus operations on gaps are independent of shifts? We can first consider relative configuration (gaps) and absolute offset. Shifting outside changes offset by ±1. Let's analyze operation sequence more structurally.

Because f_i is nondecreasing and 1-Lipschitz. Composition of t such maps. What maps are achievable in t steps? Maybe each f_i is a "folding" toward i. Composition of t f_i can be represented as g(x) = median? Let's explore.

For a single piece, repeated operations with centers c_1,...,c_t. Its position evolves by moving one step toward c_s each step. This is like "follow the leader" but center sequence same for all. For a piece at x, final position is obtained by applying transformations. There may be a known representation: The set of possible positions after t operations for a piece starting at x is [x-t, x+t] but constrained by centers? Actually any position within distance t maybe possible for a single piece by choosing centers appropriately? For one piece, to move from x to y in t steps, choose centers on the side of current position toward y each step; if y=x, choose center at current or alternate? For one piece, any y with |x-y|<=t and parity? Since each step moves by -1,0,+1 depending on center relative to current. Can choose center equal current to stay, or center beyond to move. So any y with distance <=t is possible in exactly t (with stays). For multiple pieces, centers common.

Maybe the operation is equivalent to applying a "1-Lipschitz nondecreasing map" g_t that is also "t-improper"? The example A={1,2,4}->{2,4} in 2 ops. The final map g after sequence i=1 then i=4:
g(1)=2, g(2)=2, g(4)=4. This is nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz? Check g(1)=2,g(2)=2 diff0; g(2)=2,g(4)=4 diff2, input diff2, ok. Net displacement: g(1)-1=1, g(2)-2=0, g(4)-4=0. Max net 1. But t=2. So net displacement lower bound not enough.

Maybe min operations relates to "earth mover's distance" with L1? For A={1,2,4}->B={2,4}, total movement? We need move one piece from 1 to2 (distance1) and maybe no net for others. Initial gaps: between 1-2 =1, between 2-4=2. Target gaps: between 2-4=2. Need reduce first gap from1 to0 (merge pieces at1 and2), keep second gap 2. Operation choosing i=2 (at position2) reduces both adjacent gaps by1: first gap 1->0 (merge), second gap 2->1, resulting {2,3}. That's not target because second gap shrunk. Operation choosing i=4 (outside? at max) reduces left gap 2->1, resulting {2,3,4}; not merge first. Operation choosing i=1 reduces right gap? For i=1 at min, right gap 1->0, and next gap? So choosing min shifts all pieces >min left by1, but min stays, so it effectively shifts the rest left, reducing first gap to0 and preserving other gaps? Wait if all >min shift left, the gap between min and next becomes old gap-1; gaps among shifted pieces unchanged. Thus choosing i=min merges the first two positions (if gap1) and shifts the tail left, preserving internal gaps. Then choose i=4 shifts all pieces right? From {1,3}, i=4 (outside max) shifts all right by1 -> {2,4}. So 2 ops.

This suggests operations can be understood as "choosing an endpoint" shifts the rest toward that endpoint, merging adjacent gap at that endpoint, while preserving other gaps. Choosing interior reduces gaps around center.

Maybe min operations equals minimum over "merge tree" of gaps? Let's model gaps.

Let sorted support positions p_1<...<p_m. Gaps d_r = p_{r+1}-p_r >=1 for r=1..m-1. Operation effects on gaps and offset:

Case i < p_1 (outside left): all positions shift left by1. Gaps unchanged, offset -1.
Case i > p_m (outside right): all positions shift right by1. Gaps unchanged, offset +1.
Case i = p_1 (left endpoint): p_1 stays, all others shift left by1. So p_1' = p_1, p_{r+1}' = p_{r+1}-1. New gaps:
d_1' = (p_2-1)-p_1 = d_1 -1.
For r>=2: d_r' = (p_{r+1}-1)-(p_r-1)=d_r unchanged.
If d_1=1, p_1 and p_2 merge, support size decreases; gap d_1 disappears and new first gap? Actually if merge, positions p_1 and p_2-1 = p_1, so support starts at p_1; next position p_3-1. Gap to next = (p_3-1)-p_1 = d_1+d_2-1? Wait if d_1=1, p_2=p_1+1, p_3-1 = p_1+1+d_2-1? Let's compute p_3-1 - p_1 = (p_1+d_1+d_2)-1 - p_1 = d_1+d_2-1 = d_2 if d_1=1. So gap d_2 remains. In general, choosing left endpoint reduces d_1 by1; if d_1 becomes0, remove it and combine? Actually if d_1>1, no merge, new first gap d_1-1, other gaps same. If d_1=1, merge p1,p2, new first gap = old d_2 (since d_1+d_2-1 = d_2). So effectively d_1 deleted, d_2 unchanged. So choosing left endpoint "decreases the leftmost gap by1; if it reaches 0, delete it and the next gap shifts left unchanged". Similarly choosing right endpoint decreases rightmost gap by1; if reaches0 delete it, previous gap unchanged.

Case i = p_m (right endpoint): symmetric: p_m stays, others shift right by1. d_{m-1} decreases by1; if 0 delete, previous gaps unchanged.

Case i strictly between p_r and p_{r+1}: left side shifts right, right side shifts left. Gaps:
- d_r decreases by2 (if i inside gap).
- other gaps unchanged.
If d_r=1 no integer inside; if d_r>=2. If d_r=2, merge, delete d_r, adjacent gaps? Let's compute p_r' = p_r+1, p_{r+1}'=p_{r+1}-1 = p_r+1, so merge. Gap to left p_{r-1}' = p_{r-1}+1 (if exists), so gap left' = (p_r+1)-(p_{r-1}+1)=d_{r-1} unchanged. Gap to right p_{r+2}'=p_{r+2}-1, gap right' = (p_{r+2}-1)-(p_{r+1}-1)=d_{r+1} unchanged. For any two initial pieces a_i < a_j, final positions g(a_i) <= g(a_j) and g(a_j)-g(a_i) <= a_j-a_i. This is equivalent to:
g(a_j) - a_j <= g(a_i) - a_i (h nonincreasing). So h_i = b_k - a_i is nonincreasing with i. Thus for all i<j, b_{group(i)} - a_i >= b_{group(j)} - a_j. This is the same as h nonincreasing. This condition is necessary and sufficient for existence of a nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz map g on the finite set mapping to B. It can be checked by h_i nonincreasing. Let's examine h_i = final position - initial position. For a composition of t f_i, h_i is nonincreasing, and also each h_i ∈ [-t,t]. But as seen, h=[1,0,0] with t=1 satisfies h_i∈[-1,1] and nonincreasing, yet not reachable in1. So additional constraints.

What are the constraints on h for a 1-step map? For f_i, h(x)=+1 for x<i, 0 at x=i, -1 for x>i. On the finite sorted A, h is a sequence of +1's, then possibly 0, then -1's. More precisely, there exists i such that:
- if a_j < i: h_j=+1
- if a_j = i: h_j=0
- if a_j > i: h_j=-1
Because A has no duplicates. Since i can be between positions or equal. Thus h_j ∈ {+1,0,-1}, and the sequence h is +1 for a prefix, then maybe 0 for at most one element (if i equals some a_j), then -1 for suffix. It cannot have +1,0,0 (two zeros) because only one position equals i; if i between positions, no zero, h is +1... then -1... with a drop from +1 to -1. For A={1,2,4}, h=[+1,0,0] has two zeros, impossible in 1. For t=2, h can be more complex.

Now, how to compute min t for a given h? Let's study the dynamics in terms of h and "gaps".

Let initial sorted positions a_i. Final positions g(a_i)=b_{group(i)}. Define h_i = g(a_i)-a_i. Since g is nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz, h_i is nonincreasing and h_i - h_{i+1} = (a_{i+1}-a_i) - (g(a_{i+1})-g(a_i)) = d_i - e_i, where e_i = g(a_{i+1})-g(a_i) is final distance between consecutive initial pieces (0 if same target, or target gap). Since e_i >=0 integer, h_i - h_{i+1} = d_i - e_i. Because d_i >=1 and e_i can be 0..d_i. Thus h drops by d_i - e_i, which is between 0 and d_i. For A={1,2,4}, h=[1,0,0]: drops: h1-h2=1 = d1(1)-e1(0); h2-h3=0 = d2(2)-e2(2). So h drop equals amount by which gap d_i is reduced (if e_i<d_i). If pieces in same group, e_i=0, drop=d_i. If gap preserved, drop=0.

Thus choosing grouping determines final distances e_i between consecutive initial pieces. Since final support B, e_i are either 0 (inside group) or target gaps between groups. The h sequence is determined up to a constant by h_i - h_{i+1}=d_i - e_i. The constant corresponds to overall shift. Specifically, h_i = C - sum_{r=i}^{M-1} (d_r - e_r)? Let's compute from right: h_M = g(a_M)-a_M. For i<M, h_i = h_{i+1} + d_i - e_i. So h is determined by e_i and h_M. The final positions b_k impose that for group boundaries, e_i = target gap; for internal, e_i=0. Also b_1 = a_1 + h_1 must equal target first position. Thus h_1 = b_1 - a_1. This fixes C. So for a given grouping, h is uniquely determined. The condition h nonincreasing is automatic if e_i<=d_i. Need also h_i integer (yes). Given group boundaries c_k, the final position of group k is b_k. For any piece i in group k, g(a_i)=b_k. Thus h_i = b_k - a_i. For adjacent pieces within group, e_i=0, h_i-h_{i+1}=a_{i+1}-a_i=d_i. For boundary between group k and k+1 at index c_k, e_{c_k}=b_{k+1}-b_k, h_{c_k}-h_{c_k+1}=d_{c_k}-e_{c_k}. This is >=0 iff e_{c_k} <= d_{c_k}. Maybe there is a "height" function whose value at each piece changes by at most1 per operation? Let's define h_i = current position - initial position. Therefore each operation adds +1 to a prefix of h, -1 to a suffix, and 0 to maybe one middle element. This is key! Because current positions p_i are sorted, the condition p_i < c defines a prefix. Thus h update is:
- choose an index r (number of pieces with p_i < c) and s (number with p_i > c), with r+s <= M (if c equals some p, one zero).
- h_i += 1 for i <= r (prefix)
- h_i -= 1 for i > M-s (suffix)
- h_i unchanged for middle (if any at c).
This is independent of actual positions except which prefix/suffix. Since p_i sorted, yes.

So the dynamics of h (displacement from initial positions) under an operation is: add +1 to a prefix, -1 to a suffix, and 0 to the middle (possibly empty). The prefix and suffix are disjoint and cover all except maybe one index where current position equals center. But can we choose any prefix/suffix? If c < p_1: all p_i > c, so all h_i -=1 (prefix length0, suffix M).
If c > p_M: all p_i < c, so all h_i +=1 (prefix M, suffix0).
If p_r < c < p_{r+1}: i<=r have p_i<c -> +1; i>=r+1 have p_i>c -> -1. So prefix r, suffix M-r, no zero.
If c = p_r: i<r have p_i<c -> +1; i=r zero; i>r have p_i>c -> -1. So prefix r-1, suffix M-r, zero at r.
Thus each operation updates h by:
- Choose r from 0..M (prefix length) and s from 0..M-r (suffix length), with if r+s < M then the middle index (r+1) is zero and must satisfy p_{r+1}=c, which is always possible by choosing c=p_{r+1}; if r+s=M no zero. M=2: initial a1<a2. Target support K=1 or2.
If K=2: bijection t1=b1,t2=b2. h=[b1-a1, b2-a2]. Need h nonincreasing: h1>=h2. This is equivalent b2-b1 <= a2-a1. What is min ops?
Let's enumerate possible operations for two pieces. Current positions p1<=p2. Choose center c:
- c < p1: both move left: h both -1.
- c > p2: both move right: h both +1.
- c = p1: p1 stays, p2 moves left: h1 +=0, h2 -=1.
- c = p2: p1 moves right, p2 stays: h1 +=1, h2 +=0.
- p1 < c < p2: p1 moves right, p2 moves left: h1 +=1, h2 -=1.
So the difference Δ = h1 - h2 (nonincreasing? h1>=h2) changes:
- shift both: Δ unchanged.
- c=p1: h1 same, h2-1 => Δ +1.
- c=p2: h1+1, h2 same => Δ +1.
- c between: h1+1, h2-1 => Δ +2.
Thus Δ can increase by 0,1,2 per operation. Target Δ = (b1-a1)-(b2-a2) = (a2-a1) - (b2-b1) = d_initial - d_target >=0. We need increase Δ from0 to D. Min ops at least ceil(D/2). Also h1 and h2 individually need reach target values. But maybe min ops = max(ceil(D/2), |?|).

Starting h=0. An interior reduction of gap i by2: h_j +=1 for j<=i, h_j -=1 for j>i. This changes h by +1 on prefix, -1 on suffix. An adjacent pair reduction (center at piece k) reduces gaps k-1 and k by1: h_j +=1 for j<k, h_j -=1 for j>k, h_k unchanged. This is +1 prefix up to k-1, -1 suffix from k+1. Endpoint left reduces gap1 by1: h_1 unchanged, h_j -=1 for j>1. Endpoint right: h_j +=1 for j<M, h_M unchanged. Global shift: all h +=1 or -1.

All these operations have the form: choose a split point; add +1 to a prefix, -1 to a suffix, with possibly one zero at the split (if center at a piece) or no zero (if center inside gap/outside). In fact, every operation is exactly this form. The effect on h is a "step" function: +1 on a prefix, -1 on a suffix, 0 on a middle block (possibly empty). The reductions r_i = h_i - h_{i+1} (drops) are affected by the difference between adjacent h.

Maybe we can decompose h into operations by considering h_M and Δ. Since h_i = h_M + sum_{j=i}^{M-1} Δ_j. Target h determined by Δ and h_M. Operations add to Δ and h_M. We need reach target Δ, h_M from 0. Each operation:
- Type U_r (center in gap after r, including outside): Δ_r +=2, h_M -=1 (for r<M), or h_M +=1 for r=M (all +1).
- Type W_k (center at piece k): Δ_{k-1} +=1 (if k>1), Δ_k +=1 (if k<M), h_M += -1 if k<M, 0 if k=M.
But wait is h_M always updated by -1 for U_r r<M? Let's verify with operation center in gap after r: pieces >r move left, so the rightmost piece h_M decreases by1. Yes. Center at piece k<M: rightmost piece >k moves left, h_M decreases by1. Center at piece M: rightmost stays, h_M unchanged. Center outside left (r=0): all pieces move left, h_M decreases by1. Center outside right (r=M): all move right, h_M increases by1. So indeed.

Thus each operation either:
- Increases h_M by +1 (only shift right all pieces), with no Δ.
- Decreases h_M by 1 (all other operations), while adding 2 to one Δ (gap center) or 1 to two adjacent Δ (piece center).
This is a key simplification! Because the rightmost piece's displacement h_M changes by -1 for every operation except a rightward global shift. To end with target h_M, the number of operations t and number of right shifts s satisfy: h_M = s - (t-s) = 2s - t, so s = (t + h_M)/2. Thus t ≡ h_M mod 2 and t >= |h_M|. The other operations (t-s) must generate the target differences Δ_i. Each non-right-shift operation contributes a "unit" to Δ: either 2 to a single gap (center inside gap) or 1 to two adjacent gaps (center at piece). This is exactly like we have to build the multiset of Δ_i using operations that add 2 to one gap or 1 to two adjacent gaps. The number of non-right-shift operations is q = t - s = (t - h_M)/2. We need q operations to produce Δ. Additionally, the right shifts don't affect Δ.

But wait what about left global shift (u_0) is included as non-right-shift with Δ_0? It decreases h_M by1 and no Δ? Earlier u_0 (center outside left) all pieces move left, h_M -=1, no Δ. Did we include? In gap center type U_r with r=0: Δ_0? There is no gap before first; it adds 2 to Δ_0? Let's define Δ_0 = h_1 - h_0? Not. Actually center outside left (c < p_1) moves all pieces left, so h_i -=1 for all i. This decreases h_M by1 and also decreases all h_i by1, so differences Δ_i unchanged. It is a global left shift. In our operation types, U_0 should be global left shift: h_M -=1, no Δ. Similarly center outside right U_M is global right shift: h_M +=1, no Δ. Center in gap between p_r and p_{r+1} for 1<=r<=M-1: h_i +=1 for i<=r, -1 for i>r. This decreases h_M by1, and increases Δ_r by2. Yes. So global left shift is separate.

Center at piece k:
- k=1: h_1=0, h_i=-1 for i>1. This decreases h_M by1, increases Δ_1 by1. (No Δ_0)
- 1<k<M: h_i=+1 for i<k, 0 at k, -1 for i>k. h_M -=1, Δ_{k-1} +=1, Δ_k +=1.
- k=M: h_i=+1 for i<M, 0 at M. h_M unchanged, Δ_{M-1} +=1. (This is like a "left shift of all except rightmost", no h_M decrease.)

So the generators for Δ (ignoring h_M) are:
- For each gap r=1..M-1: add 2 to Δ_r (center inside gap), cost1, h_M -1.
- For each internal piece k=2..M-1: add 1 to Δ_{k-1} and Δ_k, cost1, h_M -1.
- For left endpoint k=1: add 1 to Δ_1, cost1, h_M -1.
- For right endpoint k=M: add 1 to Δ_{M-1}, cost1, h_M unchanged.
- Global left shift: no Δ, cost1, h_M -1.
- Global right shift: no Δ, cost1, h_M +1.

But note center at piece k is only feasible if there is a piece at that position; in support set, every k corresponds to an occupied position (the k-th piece). So yes.

Now, the target Δ_i = h_i - h_{i+1} = d_i - e_i are given nonnegative integers. We need choose a multiset of operations (excluding global shifts) that produce exactly Δ_i, and count their effect on h_M. Let q be number of non-global operations. Each non-global operation has a "h_M cost" c = 1 except right endpoint k=M has c=0. Global left shift has c=1 and no Δ; global right shift has c=-1 (increases h_M). Let total h_M = (#right shifts) - (#left shifts) - (#non-global ops with c=1) + (#right endpoint ops with c=0). Actually if we separate global shifts, non-global ops include gap centers (c=1), internal piece centers (c=1), left endpoint (c=1), right endpoint (c=0). Let q = number of non-global ops. Let s = #right global shifts, l = #left global shifts. Then h_M = s - l - q + z, where z = #right endpoint ops (c=0 instead of1). Since q = q1 + z, q1 ops with c=1. Then h_M = s - l - q1. Also total operations t = q + s + l. We can always add pairs of left/right shifts to adjust? But min t.

However, maybe we can avoid global shifts by choosing operations that also produce Δ? But global shifts may be needed to satisfy h_M and parity.

The Δ production problem: We need represent the sequence Δ_i as sum of generators:
- Type G_i: 2 at position i (gap center), cost1, c=1.
- Type P_i: 1 at positions i-1 and i for i=2..M-1 (internal piece), cost1, c=1.
- Type L: 1 at position1, cost1, c=1 (left endpoint).
- Type R: 1 at position M-1, cost1, c=0 (right endpoint).
Also global left shift: no Δ, cost1, c=1.

This is like we have a path of M-1 gaps. We need cover Δ_i with operations that add 2 to a single gap, or add 1 to two adjacent gaps (including endpoints with a dummy? L adds to gap1 only, R adds to gap M-1 only). This is exactly the minimum number of "domino" and "monomino" operations to build Δ, where a domino covers two adjacent gaps with 1, a monomino covers one gap with 2, and endpoint monomino covers one gap with1. Let's first ignore h_M cost and find minimum q to produce Δ. This is a known problem: Given nonnegative integers Δ_i, we can:
- Add 2 to any i (cost1).
- Add 1 to i and i+1 (cost1).
- Add 1 to i=1 or i=M-1 (cost1) [endpoint half-domino].
Actually L and R are just add1 to endpoint gap. Internal piece P_i adds1 to adjacent gaps; this is a domino covering gaps i-1 and i. Left endpoint L is a half-domino at gap1; right endpoint R at gap M-1. Gap center G_i adds2 to one gap (a double monomino).

Let's formalize.

Given target h (nonincreasing), define Δ_i = h_i - h_{i+1} for i=1..M-1, and h_M. We need find minimum t operations. Each operation is one of:
- Global right shift: no Δ, h_M +=1, cost1.
- Global left shift: no Δ, h_M -=1, cost1.
- Gap center i (1<=i<=M-1): Δ_i +=2, h_M -=1, cost1.
- Internal piece k (2<=k<=M-1): Δ_{k-1} +=1, Δ_k +=1, h_M -=1, cost1.
- Left endpoint piece (k=1): Δ_1 +=1, h_M -=1, cost1.
- Right endpoint piece (k=M): Δ_{M-1} +=1, h_M +=0, cost1.

Wait right endpoint piece: center at rightmost piece, left side moves right, rightmost stays. This increases h_i for i<M by1, h_M unchanged. It adds Δ_{M-1}? h_{M-1} increases by1, h_M unchanged, so Δ_{M-1} increases by1. Yes h_M cost 0. Good.

But are there operations that add 1 to Δ_1 and h_M? Left endpoint yes. What about center at piece k=1: h_1 unchanged, others -1, so Δ_1 = h1-h2 increases by1 (h2 decreases), h_M decreases by1. Good.

Now, note that any operation that is not global right shift has h_M nonincreasing (decreases by1 or0). To reach target h_M, we need enough global right shifts. But global right shifts also increase all h_i, thus don't affect Δ. So the plan: choose a multiset of non-global operations to produce Δ, with total "h_M cost" C = (#non-global ops with c=1) (right endpoint ops c=0). Then after these ops, h_M = -C (since start0, each c=1 decreases by1, c=0 no change). Then add s global right shifts to reach target h_M: s = h_M + C. We cannot use global left shifts? If h_M + C could be negative, we would need global left shifts? But we can also choose operations with c=0 or maybe global left shifts to decrease h_M. However global left shifts don't help produce Δ and increase cost; if target h_M is very negative, maybe we need them. But note non-global operations already decrease h_M; if we need more decrease, we can add global left shifts. In general, after producing Δ with q operations, let C be number of c=1 among them (q - z, where z = #right endpoint ops). Then h_M = -C - l + s, where l=#global left, s=#global right. We want h_M target. Total t = q + l + s. To minimize, we can choose l,s nonnegative satisfying s - l = h_M + C. Minimum l+s = |h_M + C|. Thus t = q + |h_M + C|. Since C depends on choice of operations to produce Δ (specifically, right endpoint ops reduce C by1 relative to q). We want minimize q + |h_M + C| over all ways to produce Δ.

For M=2, Δ=D. To produce Δ:
- Use floor(D/2) gap centers (c=1) and if D odd one endpoint op. The endpoint op could be left (c=1) or right (c=0). If choose right for odd, q = ceil(D/2), C = floor(D/2)*1 + 0 = floor(D/2). If choose left, C = ceil(D/2). Then t = q + |h_M + C|.

So the problem reduces to: Given target h (from grouping), compute minimum over operations to produce Δ_i exactly, with cost q + |h_M + C|, where C = number of non-global operations that decrease h_M (i.e., all except right endpoint piece operations). Equivalently, each operation type has:
- Gap center i: adds 2 to Δ_i, cost1, h_M contribution -1.

- Right endpoint: adds 1 to Δ_{M-1}, cost1, h_M contribution 0.
- Global shifts: no Δ, cost |...|.

Now, we need choose grouping (which initial pieces merge to which target) to minimize t. The grouping determines h and Δ. We need find global minimum over all valid groupings.

This is now a combinatorial optimization. Let's express Δ_i = h_i - h_{i+1} = d_i - e_i, where d_i = a_{i+1}-a_i (initial gap), e_i = g(a_{i+1})-g(a_i) (final distance between consecutive initial pieces). The sum of e_i over all i = a_M - a_1 - (h_1 - h_M) = (a_M-a_1) - (b_1-a_1 - (b_K-a_M)) = (a_M-a_1) - (b_1 - b_K - a_1 + a_M) = b_K - b_1. So total final span. If we assign each boundary a target gap, the sequence of nonzero e_i must be exactly the target gaps in order. This is like matching target gaps to a subsequence of initial gaps, each target gap <= chosen initial gap. Thus grouping is just selecting a subsequence of initial gaps to serve as boundaries, with each selected gap d_r >= corresponding target gap e_k (in order). The Δ_i are:
- If gap i selected as boundary k: Δ_i = d_i - e_k.
- If not selected: Δ_i = d_i.
All Δ_i >=0.

We need minimize F(Δ, h_M) = min operations to produce Δ with h_M = b_K - a_M.

Now, how to compute F efficiently for N up to 1e6? Need derive closed form.

Let's analyze the Δ production cost more abstractly.

We have M-1 gaps indexed 1..L (L=M-1). Target Δ_i are nonnegative integers. Operations to produce Δ:
1. Add 2 to a single gap i: cost1, h_cost1.
2. Add 1 to two adjacent gaps (i,i+1): cost1, h_cost1.
3. Add 1 to gap1 only: cost1, h_cost1.
4. Add 1 to gap L only: cost1, h_cost0.
(Global shifts handled separately.)

We need minimize q + |h_M + C|, where q = number of operations of types1-4, C = number of these operations with h_cost1 (i.e., all except type4). Let z = number of type4 operations (right endpoint). Then q = C + z. Total t = q + |h_M + C| = C + z + |h_M + C|.

Let C be the number of "left-cost" operations. We can think each operation has a "weight" w:
- Type1,2,3: contributes to Δ and has h_cost1.
- Type4: contributes to Δ and has h_cost0.

We need produce Δ. The final t depends on C and z. Since q = C+z, and h_M fixed.

Maybe we can reformulate by adding a dummy gap? Type4 (right endpoint) is like adding 1 to gap L with cost1 but h_cost0. Type3 (left endpoint) adds 1 to gap1 with cost1 h_cost1. Gap center adds 2 to one gap h_cost1. Internal piece adds 1 to adjacent gaps h_cost1.

Notice that h_cost1 operations are exactly those that move the rightmost piece left (or leave? type4 leaves rightmost). In terms of Δ production, type4 is a "half" at the right boundary with no h_M cost. This asymmetry arises because h_M is the rightmost displacement. If we instead considered h_1, left endpoint would be special. But h_M is fixed by target.

So the Δ production problem is nontrivial: not all Δ are producible. But since Δ comes from a valid grouping, is it always producible? The example Δ=[0,1,0] came from a grouping that seemed valid by e<=d, but actually unreachable. So additional constraints are captured by Δ producibility.

We need characterize producible Δ and compute min cost.

Let's analyze the Δ production problem fully.

We have gaps 1..L (L=M-1). Operations:
- Type A_i: add 2 to gap i (center inside gap). Let's call "double".
- Type B_i for i=1..L-1: add 1 to gaps i and i+1 (center at piece i+1). "domino".
- Type C_L? Actually left endpoint: add 1 to gap1. "left half".
- Type D: add 1 to gap L. "right half".

This is exactly representing Δ as:
Δ_i = 2 x_i + y_i,
where y_i is the degree of vertex i in a multigraph on path with vertices 1..L, edges between i and i+1 (dominoes), plus loops at vertex1 and vertexL (endpoint halves). Loops at endpoints can be multiple. Edges can be multiple. There are no loops at internal vertices. Thus y_i must be the degree sequence of a multigraph on a path with loops only at endpoints. For a path, a sequence y is realizable iff? Let's derive.

Let z_i = number of edges between vertex i and i+1 for i=1..L-1. Let l1,lL >=0 be loops at endpoints. Then:
y_1 = z_1 + l1
y_i = z_{i-1} + z_i for 2<=i<=L-1
y_L = z_{L-1} + lL

We can eliminate l1,lL: need z_1 <= y_1, z_{L-1} <= y_L, and z_i >=0 satisfying internal equations. Given y_2..y_{L-1}, the recurrence z_i = y_i - z_{i-1} for i=2..L-1 (with z_1 variable). This determines z_i as alternating sums. The existence of nonnegative z_1 and z_i plus boundary inequalities is equivalent to a set of interval constraints on z_1.

Let's compute constraints. For i=1..L-1, z_i can be expressed:
z_i = S_i + (-1)^{i-1} z_1? Let's derive.
z_2 = y_2 - z_1.
z_3 = y_3 - z_2 = y_3 - y_2 + z_1.
z_4 = y_4 - z_3 = y_4 - y_3 + y_2 - z_1.
So z_i = A_i + (-1)^{i-1} z_1, where A_i = y_i - y_{i-1} + y_{i-2} - ... ± y_2? More precisely:
z_1 = z_1.
z_2 = y_2 - z_1.
z_3 = y_3 - y_2 + z_1.

Constraints:
z_i >=0 for i=1..L-1.
z_1 <= y_1 (l1>=0).
z_{L-1} <= y_L (lL>=0).

These give lower/upper bounds on z_1 depending on parity:
For i odd: z_i = P_i + z_1 >=0 => z_1 >= -P_i.
For i even: z_i = P_i - z_1 >=0 => z_1 <= P_i.
Also z_1 <= y_1.
And for i=L-1:
- If L-1 odd: z_{L-1}=P_{L-1}+z_1 <= y_L => z_1 <= y_L - P_{L-1}.
- If L-1 even: z_{L-1}=P_{L-1}-z_1 <= y_L => z_1 >= P_{L-1} - y_L.

Thus a feasible z_1 exists iff lower <= upper. Since l1,lL can be arbitrarily large? Actually z_1 <= y_1, y_1 can be large. Loops can absorb extra, so no upper from y1 except z1<=y1; l1 = y1-z1. Similarly lL = y_L - z_{L-1} >=0 gives bound. So if feasible, we can choose z1.

Now, the cost q for a given decomposition:
q = sum_i x_i + sum_{i=1}^{L-1} z_i + l1 + lL.
Because each double op x_i, each domino z_i, each left loop l1, right loop lL.
And h_cost C = q - lL (since right loops have h_cost0, all others h_cost1). Actually lL is type4, h_cost0. So C = sum x_i + sum z_i + l1.

We need minimize q + |h_M + C| = (C + lL) + |h_M + C|.

This is an optimization over x_i >=0, y_i = Δ_i - 2x_i >=0, and a feasible domino/loop representation of y.

Maybe there is an even simpler global formula that avoids grouping optimization. Let's think.

The grouping chooses which initial gaps become boundaries and sets Δ_i = d_i - e_k for boundaries, else d_i. Since d_i are initial gaps (>=1). Target gaps e_k are final gaps. Actually if a gap is not selected as boundary, e_i=0, so Δ_i = d_i, meaning the entire initial gap is eliminated (pieces merge). If selected, Δ_i = d_i - e_k, meaning the gap is reduced to target gap.

Thus Δ_i are the amounts by which each initial gap is reduced. The sum of Δ_i = (a_M-a_1) - (b_K-b_1) = initial span - target span. This is the total reduction in span. Also h_M = b_K - a_M.

This flat-step theory might be the key! Let's formalize.

For any nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz map g: Z -> Z, define flat steps F(g) = {x ∈ Z | g(x+1)=g(x)}. Since g(x+1)-g(x) ∈ {0,1}. The number of flat steps can be finite if g(x)=x+c outside some interval? Composition of t f_i: slope 1 at infinities? Actually if x very negative, all centers to right, each f_i moves right by1, so g(x)=x+t. If x very positive, g(x)=x-t. Thus h(x)=g(x)-x tends to +t on left, -t on right. So h has range 2t and flat steps count 2t? For f_i, h from +1 to -1, two flats. For t=2, h from +2 to -2, four flats. So indeed each operation adds two flat steps, and the total number of flat steps is exactly 2t. This is a strong invariant.

Maybe we can avoid flat theory and solve the production model directly with a greedy algorithm for F.

Let's return to the Δ production model. The operations to produce Δ are exactly placing dominoes (flat pairs) and doubles? Wait in the flat representation, each operation f_i adds a domino of flats at positions i-1 and i. The flat set after t operations is a union of t dominoes (adjacent pairs), but dominoes can overlap? The production model with operations type1 (gap center adds 2 to Δ_i) corresponds to placing a domino entirely within the interval between piece i and i+1? Type2 (internal piece) corresponds to a domino crossing a piece position? Let's map.

A flat position x is an integer between? For a piece at position a_i, h_i = t - count(F < a_i). The difference Δ_i = count(F in [a_i, a_{i+1})). A domino (x,x+1) contributes 1 to the count of flats at x and at x+1. If both x and x+1 lie in the same interval [a_i,a_{i+1}), it contributes 2 to Δ_i. If x = a_i -? Let's see intervals are [a_i, a_{i+1}) over integer positions. A domino can have one flat in interval i and one in interval i+1 if it crosses a piece position a_{i+1}. Since flat positions are integers, crossing at a piece position means one flat at a_{i+1}-1 and one at a_{i+1}? Actually interval i is [a_i, a_{i+1}) includes a_{i+1}-1 but not a_{i+1}. Interval i+1 starts at a_{i+1}. A domino (x,x+1) with x=a_{i+1}-1 has flats at a_{i+1}-1 (interval i) and a_{i+1} (interval i+1). This contributes 1 to Δ_i and 1 to Δ_{i+1}. This corresponds to center at piece i+1 (internal piece) in production model! A domino entirely inside interval i contributes 2 to Δ_i, corresponding to gap center. What about left endpoint operation (center at leftmost piece) corresponds to a domino before a_1? Right endpoint corresponds to after a_M. Yes! This is exactly the same as the production model but with dominoes on the integer line. The flat set F is a union of t dominoes; each domino contributes to Δ counts depending on its position relative to piece positions. The number of dominoes before a_1 contributes to c_1 = t - h_1, and after a_M contributes to c_{M+1} = t + h_M.

Thus the problem is: Place t dominoes (adjacent pairs of integers) on the line. Let for each interval between consecutive pieces I_i = [a_i, a_{i+1}) contain exactly Δ_i flat positions. Let before a_1 contain c_1 = t - h_1 flats, after a_M contain c_{M+1} = t + h_M flats. Since each domino contributes 2 flats total, total flats = 2t. The counts before/inside/after must sum to 2t. This automatically holds if c_1 + sum Δ_i + c_{M+1} = (t-h1)+(h1-hM)+(t+hM)=2t.

Now, placing t dominoes to achieve given interval counts is a classic problem. A domino covers two adjacent integer positions. It contributes:
- 2 to one interval if both positions inside same interval.
- 1 to each of two adjacent intervals if it crosses a boundary between intervals (i.e., at a piece position).
- 2 to "before" if both before a1, etc.
- 1 to before and interval1 if crosses a1, etc.

This is exactly the production model with:
- Domino entirely inside interval i: adds 2 to Δ_i (gap center).
- Domino crossing boundary between interval i and i+1 (at piece a_{i+1}): adds 1 to Δ_i and Δ_{i+1} (internal piece).
- Domino crossing before a1 and interval1: adds 1 to c_1 and Δ_1 (left endpoint).
- Domino crossing interval L and after a_M: adds 1 to Δ_L and c_{M+1} (right endpoint).
- Domino entirely before a1: adds 2 to c_1 (global left shift? Actually flat before a1 affects h all pieces? If a domino entirely before a1, it contributes 2 to c_1, increasing t? It corresponds to global left shift? Let's see.)
- Domino entirely after a_M: adds 2 to c_{M+1} (global right shift?).

In our previous production model, global shifts were operations that don't affect Δ and adjust h_M. In flat terms, a global right shift corresponds to a domino entirely after a_M? Let's check: Operation center > all pieces (global right shift) adds a domino of flats to the right of all pieces, increasing c_{M+1} by2 and t by1, h_M increases by1. Yes. Global left shift corresponds to domino entirely before a1, increasing c_1 by2 and t by1, h_M decreases by1? If t increases by1 and c_1 increases by2, h_1 = t - c_1 decreases by1; h_M = t - c_M? c_M = c_1+sumΔ, so h_M decreases by1. Good.

Thus the flat/domino model unifies everything: We need choose t dominoes on the integer line such that the number of dominoes crossing each boundary between regions (before a1, intervals I_i, after a_M) satisfies the required counts. The regions are M+1: region0 = (-∞, a1), region i = [a_i, a_{i+1}) for i=1..M-1, region M = [a_M, ∞). Required flat counts in region i: r_0 = c_1 = t - h_1, r_i = Δ_i for 1<=i<=M-1, r_M = c_{M+1} = t + h_M. Note r_i are nonnegative. Each domino covers two adjacent integer positions, so it either lies entirely in one region (contributing 2 to that region) or crosses exactly one boundary between adjacent regions (contributing 1 to each). It cannot cross more than one boundary because length2. Thus the problem becomes: Given required counts r_i (number of flat positions in each region), can we place t dominoes such that each region i has r_i covered positions? Let x_i = number of dominoes entirely in region i (contributing 2 to r_i). Let y_i = number of dominoes crossing boundary between region i-1 and i (for i=1..M) (contributing 1 to r_{i-1} and r_i). Then:
r_i = 2 x_i + y_i + y_{i+1}, for i=0..M, with y_0=y_{M+1}=0? Boundaries: between region0 and1 is y_1; between region i-1 and i is y_i for i=1..M; between region M-1 and M is y_M. So:
r_0 = 2 x_0 + y_1
r_i = 2 x_i + y_i + y_{i+1} for 1<=i<=M-1
r_M = 2 x_M + y_M
Total dominoes t = sum_{i=0}^M x_i + sum_{i=1}^M y_i.
This is exactly the production model with x_i doubles, y_i crossing dominoes. The h_cost C? In flat model, t is directly the number of dominoes, and h_M = r_M - t? Since r_M = t + h_M => h_M = r_M - t. But we don't need separate cost; t is the answer. We need find minimum t such that there exist nonnegative integers x_i,y_i satisfying these equations for some grouping (which determines r_i). But note r_i depend on t: r_0 = t - h_1, r_M = t + h_M. The internal r_i = Δ_i are independent of t. Thus t appears in boundary region counts.

This is much cleaner! Let's verify with examples.

So the problem reduces to: For each possible grouping (which determines h and Δ), find minimum t satisfying the domino region equations. But maybe we can combine grouping and t by considering the required r_i directly in terms of initial/target positions.

Let's express r_i for a given grouping:
- For region 0 (before first piece): r_0 = t - h_1 = t - (b_1 - a_1).
- For internal region i (between a_i and a_{i+1}): r_i = Δ_i = d_i - e_i, where d_i = a_{i+1}-a_i, e_i = final distance between consecutive initial pieces (0 if same group, target gap if boundary).
- For region M (after last piece): r_M = t + h_M = t + (b_K - a_M).

Notice r_0 and r_M depend on t and the first/last target positions. The internal r_i are reductions of initial gaps.

The domino equations are linear. We can eliminate x_i and y_i to find a condition on r_i. This is a flow/parity condition on a path. Let's solve the feasibility of region counts r_i with t dominoes.

Given r_i (i=0..M), we need nonnegative integers x_i, y_i satisfying:
r_0 = 2x_0 + y_1
r_i = 2x_i + y_i + y_{i+1} (1<=i<=M-1)
r_M = 2x_M + y_M
and t = sum x_i + sum y_i.

But note t is not independent; it equals total flats/2 = (sum r_i)/2. Since each domino contributes 2 flats, sum r_i = 2t. This is exactly the problem of representing r as degree sequence of a multigraph on a path with loops at vertices? Actually regions are vertices, dominoes are edges between adjacent regions or loops within a region (contributing 2). A domino entirely in region i is a loop at vertex i contributing 2 to r_i. A domino crossing boundary i is an edge between vertex i-1 and i contributing1 to each. We need a multigraph on a path with loops allowed at every vertex, where each loop contributes 2 to its vertex degree. The degree sequence r_i must be realizable. For a path with loops allowed, what are the conditions? Since loops contribute even degrees, the parity of r_i must be covered by edges (crossing dominoes). Edges form a multigraph on a path; the degree sequence from edges alone, call y-degree: for vertex i, edge degree = y_i + y_{i+1} (with y_0=y_{M+1}=0). Loops can add any even amount to each vertex. Therefore, r_i is realizable iff there exist nonnegative integers y_i (edges between i-1 and i) such that:
- y_i + y_{i+1} ≡ r_i (mod 2) for all i (with y_0=y_{M+1}=0),
- y_i + y_{i+1} <= r_i (since loops add nonnegative even),
and then x_i = (r_i - y_i - y_{i+1})/2 >=0.
This is exactly the earlier y representation, but now loops allowed at all vertices (not just endpoints). Wait in the flat model, a domino entirely inside any region is a loop at that vertex, contributing 2 to r_i. In the production model, we had doubles x_i for internal gaps, and global shifts for boundary regions as loops. Yes.

Thus feasibility condition: The parity sequence r_i mod2 must be representable as the cut? For a path, the edge variables y_i (i=1..M) satisfy:
r_0 = y_1 (mod2) (since 2x0 even)
r_i = y_i + y_{i+1} (mod2) for 1<=i<=M-1
r_M = y_M (mod2)
This is a linear system over GF(2). It has a unique solution for y_i parity:
y_1 ≡ r_0
y_2 ≡ r_1 - y_1 ≡ r_1 + r_0
y_3 ≡ r_2 + y_2 ≡ r_2 + r_1 + r_0
...
In general, y_i ≡ prefix parity P_{i-1} = r_0 + r_1 + ... + r_{i-1} (mod2). The last equation requires y_M ≡ r_M, i.e., P_{M-1} ≡ r_M, which is equivalent to sum_{i=0}^M r_i even. Now, the grouping optimization. We need choose group boundaries (which gaps are boundaries) to minimize t. Recall for a gap i:
- If it is a boundary between group k and k+1, then e_i = target gap E_k = b_{k+1}-b_k, and r_i = d_i - E_k. This can be zero if d_i = E_k.
- If it is internal (pieces merge), e_i=0, r_i = d_i >0.

Thus r_i is always positive for internal gaps. Zero r_i can only occur at boundaries where the initial gap exactly equals the target gap. If d_i < E_k, invalid (r_i negative). If d_i > E_k, r_i positive. If d_i = E_k, r_i=0 and imposes parity condition.

The target gaps E_k are the gaps between consecutive 1s in B. We need match each target gap to a distinct initial gap in order, with d_i >= E_k. Internal gaps (not matched) have r_i = d_i. This is like aligning target gaps to a subsequence of initial gaps. The h sequence is determined by the matching. We need minimize t = max(h1, -hM) adjusted by parity for matched gaps with equality.

Maybe we can compute the minimal t directly without enumerating all matchings? There are K-1 target gaps, K<=M up to 1e6. We need choose a subsequence of initial gaps of length K-1 to match target gaps in order, with d_i >= E_k. This is a standard sequence alignment. The cost t depends on h1 and hM and parity of h_i at equality matches.

Let's express h_i in terms of the matching. Let initial positions a_1<...<a_M. Target positions b_1<...<b_K. Suppose we choose boundary indices c_1<...<c_{K-1} (between a_{c_k} and a_{c_k+1}) for target gaps. Then:
- h_M = b_K - a_M.
- h_1 = b_1 - a_1.
- For any piece i, h_i = h_M + sum_{j=i}^{M-1} Δ_j, where Δ_j = d_j - E_k if j=c_k, else d_j.
But there is a simpler formula: For group k, the rightmost piece index is c_k (with c_K=M). The leftmost is c_{k-1}+1 (c_0=0). The target position b_k. For any piece i in group k, h_i = b_k - a_i. Thus we need solve the matching optimization properly.

Let's formulate the matching problem in terms of target gaps and initial gaps.

We have initial gaps d_1..d_{M-1}, target gaps E_1..E_{K-1}. Need choose indices c_1<...<c_{K-1} such that d_{c_k} >= E_k. The cost t = smallest >= t0 with parity constraints from c_k where d_{c_k}=E_k: require t ≡ h_{c_k} mod2, where h_{c_k} = b_k - a_{c_k}. But h_{c_k} can be expressed in terms of cumulative sums.

Let's define positions:
a_i = a_1 + sum_{r=1}^{i-1} d_r.
b_k = b_1 + sum_{r=1}^{k-1} E_r.
For a boundary c_k, h_{c_k} = b_k - a_{c_k} = (b_1 - a_1) + sum_{r=1}^{k-1} E_r - sum_{r=1}^{c_k-1} d_r.
Let H0 = b_1 - a_1 = h1. Then h_{c_k} = H0 + S_E(k-1) - S_d(c_k-1), where S_E prefix of target gaps, S_d prefix of initial gaps.

If d_{c_k} = E_k, then note S_d(c_k) - S_d(c_k-1) = S_E(k) - S_E(k-1). The parity of h_{c_k} can also be related to the "offset" after matching.

We need determine if there exists a subsequence c_k such that:
1. d_{c_k} >= E_k.
2. For all k with d_{c_k}=E_k, the parity of H0 + S_E(k-1) - S_d(c_k-1) equals p (where p is t mod2).

This is like finding a path in a grid from (0,0) to (M-1,K-1) with steps? Actually matching gaps is equivalent to aligning the sequence of initial gaps to target gaps, where we can skip initial gaps (merge pieces) but cannot skip target gaps. This is a standard subsequence matching. We can process target gaps left to right, choosing an initial gap index. This is equivalent to a path where we advance through initial gaps, and at certain points match a target gap.

We need know for each possible parity p whether there exists a matching with all equalities having parity p (or no equalities). Since p is just t mod2, and t0 fixed, we can compute the set of possible parities of equality matches in some matching.

Maybe we can use dynamic programming with states "current target gap index" and "parity of last equality"? But K up to 1e6, M up to1e6, need O(N).

Observe that equality d_i = E_k is a special case. If d_i > E_k, no constraint. To avoid constraints, we would prefer to match each target gap to a strictly larger initial gap. If possible, answer t0. If not, we need handle unavoidable equalities. Perhaps the only unavoidable equality occurs when the total number of initial gaps with d_i >= E_k is exactly the number of remaining target gaps? This is like Hall's condition.

Maybe we can compute the minimal t by considering the "tight" matching from the right? Let's think.

Since target gaps must be matched in order, the standard greedy from left (earliest feasible) gives the lexicographically smallest matching. Greedy from right gives lexicographically largest. If there exists any matching with no equality of bad parity, maybe we can find it by greedy with preference for d_i > E_k.

We can model each target gap E_k as needing to be matched to an initial gap d_i >= E_k. If we want to avoid equality, we need d_i > E_k. If no such gap is available in any matching, equality unavoidable. But if equality unavoidable, its parity might be forced.

Let's consider a simpler approach: Since N sum 1e6, we could perform DP over target gaps with two parities using "next greater/equal" arrays. For each target gap E_k, we need choose an initial gap index. The condition d_i >= E_k. We can precompute for each position the next index with d_i >= E_k and d_i > E_k. Then a matching exists iff we can greedily choose. To track parity constraints, we can do DP with states 0/1 for the parity of the last equality? But equality constraints require all equalities have same parity p; if we allow both parities, we can maintain possible p values.

Actually the final t parity p is chosen after matching. If a matching has equalities of both parities, it's invalid for any p. So we need matchings where all equality parities are equal (or no equalities). We can compute for each possible p whether there exists a matching with all equalities parity p.

This is a regular language over the sequence of chosen gaps: each chosen gap has type:
- L (large): d_i > E_k, no constraint.
- E0: d_i = E_k and h_i parity 0.
- E1: d_i = E_k and h_i parity 1.
We need a matching where all E labels are same (or none). We can do DP with state = parity of E labels seen so far, or a flag "none yet". Since K up to1e6, O(K * states) possible if we can transition quickly.

But the number of possible initial gaps for each target gap can be many. We need efficient transitions. This is like matching with inequalities; we can use greedy plus DP? Now, how to check existence of matching with a given parity constraint efficiently?

We can view this as a subsequence matching problem with labels. For each target gap E_k, when matched to initial gap i:
- If d_i > E_k: label is "free" (no constraint).
- If d_i = E_k: label is parity h_i mod2.
We need a matching where all labels are either free or p (for p=0 or1). Equivalently, we are forbidden from matching any equality with parity 1-p. So for a given p, we need match all target gaps using initial gaps that are either > E_k or = E_k with parity p. If this is possible, then p is feasible.

This is a standard subsequence existence with allowed sets depending on E_k. We can check by greedy: For each target gap in order, choose the earliest available initial gap that is allowed (d_i > E_k or (d_i == E_k and parity p)). If greedy succeeds, matching exists. Is greedy correct for subsequence existence with arbitrary allowed sets? Yes, for matching a sequence of queries to a sequence of items in order, where each query has a set of allowed items, the greedy algorithm that picks the earliest allowed item for each query is optimal: if any matching exists, the greedy matching (earliest possible) leaves the maximum remaining suffix, so it cannot hurt future queries. This is standard. So we can check feasibility for p in O(M+K) by scanning.

We need do this for p0 and p1. O(N) each is fine (2 passes per test, total 2e6). We also need check existence of any matching (>=) which is just greedy with allowed d_i >= E_k; but if p0 or p1 succeeds, then matching exists. However if both fail, maybe no matching at all? Since any matching has equalities of parity 0 or1 (or no equalities). No equalities is allowed for both p? Wait if no equality, then all matched gaps are >E, so it is allowed for both p. Thus if a matching exists with no equality, both p checks succeed. If a matching exists with equalities all parity0, p0 succeeds. If all parity1, p1 succeeds. If equalities mixed, neither p succeeds, but a matching exists (with >=). So we need distinguish "no matching at all" from "mixed parity only". The p checks will both fail for mixed parity; but we should output -1. So we can just run p0 and p1 checks. If either succeeds, answer accordingly. If both fail, output -1. Does this correctly handle no matching? Yes, both fail. But wait, if there is a matching with mixed parity, could there also be one with no equality? Then p checks would succeed. If not, -1. Good.

So the algorithm is:
For each test case:
- Parse A,B, get positions arrays a (length M), b (length K). If K>M? Since support size nonincreasing, if K>M impossible. But also matching target gaps requires K-1 <= M-1, so K<=M. If K>M output -1.
- Compute t0 = max(0, b[0]-a[0], a[-1]-b[-1]).
- Compute initial gaps d_i = a[i+1]-a[i] for i=0..M-2.
- Compute target gaps E_j = b[j+1]-b[j] for j=0..K-2.
- Need for each initial gap i, its index in a? For parity h_i when matched to target gap j: h_i = b[j] - a[i]. But note i is 0-based piece index left of gap between a[i] and a[i+1]. In earlier notation gap i (1-based) between piece i and i+1, h_i = b_k - a_i. So for gap index i (0-based), if matched to target gap j (between b[j] and b[j+1]), h = b[j] - a[i]. Parity = (b[j] - a[i]) & 1.
- For a given parity p, allowed gap i for target gap j if:
   d[i] > E[j] OR (d[i] == E[j] AND ((b[j] - a[i]) & 1) == p).
- Greedy: pointer ptr=0 over initial gaps. For each target gap j:
   while ptr < len(d) and not allowed(ptr, j): ptr +=1
   if ptr == len(d): fail
   ptr +=1
If succeed, feasible for p.

Now, we need implement the greedy check efficiently. For each p, we scan target gaps and advance pointer through initial gaps. The allowed condition uses d[i] and parity h = b[j] - a[i]. We can compute on the fly.

Complexity: For each test case, we may run greedy twice. Each greedy scans target gaps and advances pointer at most M. O(M+K) per p, total O(2N) per test, sum 2e6, fine.

But we must be careful: The greedy for p should allow matching a target gap to an initial gap with d_i > E_j OR (d_i == E_j and parity p). If d_i < E_j, not allowed. Since d_i and E_j are positive integers.

Now, let's revisit the derivation of t0 and parity from the flat model to ensure no missing constraints.

We had r_i = number of flats in interval I_i. For a given matching, internal r_i = d_i - E_j if boundary, or d_i if internal. Since d_i >=1, internal r_i>0. Boundary r_i can be0 if d_i=E_j. The feasibility condition for the flat set with region counts r_i was: for every i with r_i=0, prefix parity before i even. Prefix parity before i = (t - h_i) mod2. Thus t ≡ h_i mod2. We didn't consider r_i=0 at boundaries where d_i=E_j; if r_i=0, then h_i = h_{i+1}. The prefix parity condition is necessary and sufficient for existence of an even-run flat set with those region counts. We should verify sufficiency with an example where r_i=0 and prefix parity even. A={1,3,4}, B=[2,3,4], t=2, matching d1 large, d2 equality parity0. r0=t-h1=1, r1=d1-E1=1, r2=0, r3=t+hM=2. Now, the matching existence with p check: We need ensure that if greedy for p succeeds, then the resulting matching has all equality parities p. Greedy may choose a large gap (d>E) which is unconstrained, and equality gaps with parity p. It will never choose equality with parity 1-p because not allowed. Thus yes.

Input T up to 2e5, sum N 1e6. Need fast I/O.

For each test:
- Read N (int), A string, B string.
- Build lists a = [i+1 for i,ch in enumerate(A) if ch=='1'] (1-based positions).
- b = [i+1 for i,ch in enumerate(B) if ch=='1'].
- M=len(a), K=len(b).
- If K > M: print(-1); continue. (Support size cannot increase; also matching gaps impossible.)
- If K == 1: answer = max(0, b[0]-a[0], a[-1]-b[0])? Wait hM = bK - aM = b[0]-a[-1], so -hM = a[-1]-b[0]. t0 = max(0, b[0]-a[0], a[-1]-b[0]). This equals max distance to b0. Print t0. But our general algorithm with no target gaps would also work if we skip matching and print t0. We can handle K==1 separately or let matching loop over zero gaps and return t0. Simpler: if K==1, print(t0); continue.
- Compute t0 = max(0, b[0]-a[0], a[-1]-b[-1]).
- Build d = [a[i+1]-a[i] for i in range(M-1)].
- Build E = [b[j+1]-b[j] for j in range(K-1)].
- Define function feasible(p):
    ptr = 0
    L = len(d)
    for j, Ej in enumerate(E):
        # find next gap i >= ptr allowed
        while ptr < L:
            di = d[ptr]
            if di > Ej:
                break
            if di == Ej:
                # parity h = b[j] - a[ptr]
                if ((b[j] - a[ptr]) & 1) == p:
                    break
            # di < Ej or equality wrong parity
            ptr += 1
        if ptr == L:
            return False
        ptr += 1
    return True
- p0 = t0 & 1
- if feasible(p0): print(t0)
- elif feasible(1-p0): print(t0+1)
- else: print(-1)

Now, let's ensure the greedy feasible(p) doesn't need to consider that a large gap d_i > E_j might later be needed as equality for a smaller target gap? Since we process target gaps in order, choosing the earliest allowed gap is always optimal for subsequence existence. If we choose a large gap now, it might consume a gap that could have been equality for a later target, but if a later target needs it, greedy would have been able to skip it now? Wait if d_i > E_j, it's allowed for current target. Could there be a situation where skipping this large gap and using a later gap for current target allows a later target to use this large gap, and this is necessary? Greedy theorem says no: choosing the earliest possible match leaves the maximum remaining suffix. If the earliest allowed gap is d_i, any matching that uses a later gap for current target leaves a suffix starting after that later gap, which is a subset of the suffix after d_i. Thus if a matching exists using later gap, the greedy matching can be extended? Need be careful because the allowed sets for future depend on target values, but the remaining items after d_i include the later gap plus more, so any future matching after later gap is also valid after d_i (since d_i is before later gap, but we already consumed d_i for current target; future targets can still use later gap and beyond). Thus greedy is safe. Standard.

Now, let's attempt to prove the flat set condition more rigorously for my own confidence.

We have a map g reachable in t operations. Define F = {x | g(x+1)=g(x)}. Since each f_i has F={i-1,i}, and composition of nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz maps: F(g∘h) = F(h) ∪ h^{-1}(F(g))? Actually flat steps of composition = flat steps of h plus preimages under h of flat steps of g. The number of flat steps is additive? We observed for f0,f1, |F|=4 =2+2. Is it always additive? Let's prove: For nondecreasing 1-Lipschitz h, the preimage of a point under h is an interval. If g has a flat step at y (g(y+1)=g(y)), then h^{-1}({y}) and h^{-1}({y+1})? Wait flat steps of g are positions y where g(y+1)=g(y). For composition g∘h, flat at x if h(x+1)=h(x) (flat of h) OR h(x+1)=h(x)+1 and g(h(x)+1)=g(h(x)) (flat of g at h(x)). Since h(x+1)-h(x) is 0 or1. The number of x with h(x+1)=h(x)+1 and g flat at h(x) equals the number of y in F(g) such that h crosses from y to y+1 at some x. Since h is surjective and nondecreasing, for each y, there is exactly one x with h(x)=y and h(x+1)=y+1? Not exactly if h has plateaus. But the total number of such crossings plus flats of h might equal |F(h)| + |F(g)|. This is a known property of the "rank" of 1-Lipschitz maps. So |F|=2t. Also F has even runs because each operation adds a domino and composition preserves the even-run property? We saw f0,f1 produced run length4. Likely true.

Conversely, given any finite F with even runs, can construct g with that F, and decompose into t operations by taking the runs and applying centers? For a run of even length, say positions l..r with r-l+1 even, the corresponding g has h dropping by1 at each position in the run. This map can be realized by (r-l+1)/2 operations with centers l+1, l+3, ..., r? Let's test run {1,2,3,4} realized by centers1,2? Actually centers1,2 produced run {1,2,3,4}. The centers were 1 and2, not l+1,l+3. For run {0,1,4,5} realized by centers1,4. So each domino pair in the run corresponds to a center at the right endpoint of the pair? For run {1,2,3,4}, pairs (1,2) center2? and (3,4) center4? But centers used1,2. Hmm. Maybe any even run can be decomposed into adjacent pairs and each pair center = right endpoint of pair; for run {1,2,3,4}, pairs (1,2) center2, (3,4) center4. Would centers2,4 produce run? Let's test f2 then f4 maybe. Compute quickly? Likely. So any even-run set is realizable. We'll trust.

Given the complexity, the final code will implement the greedy algorithm. Need ensure no off-by-one in indices.

Let's write code carefully.

Pseudo:

import sys
def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        A = input().strip()
        B = input().strip()
        a = [i+1 for i,c in enumerate(A) if c=='1']
        b = [i+1 for i,c in enumerate(B) if c=='1']
        M = len(a); K = len(b)
        if K > M:
            out.append("-1")
            continue
        t0 = max(0, b[0]-a[0], a[-1]-b[-1])
        if K == 1:
            out.append(str(t0))
            continue
        d = [a[i+1]-a[i] for i in range(M-1)]
        E = [b[j+1]-b[j] for j in range(K-1)]
        L = len(d)
        def feasible(p):
            ptr = 0
            # For each target gap j
            for j, Ej in enumerate(E):
                # bj = b[j] (left target position)
                bj = b[j]
                while ptr < L:
                    di = d[ptr]
                    if di > Ej:
                        break
                    if di == Ej:
                        # parity of h = bj - a[ptr]
                        if ((bj - a[ptr]) & 1) == p:
                            break
                    ptr += 1
                if ptr == L:
                    return False
                ptr += 1
            return True
        p0 = t0 & 1
        if feasible(p0):
            out.append(str(t0))
        elif feasible(p0 ^ 1):
            out.append(str(t0+1))
        else:
            out.append("-1")
    print("\n".join(out))

Need be careful: In feasible, when di > Ej, we break and match this gap. But what if di > Ej but this gap is before ptr? ptr ensures we don't go back. Good.

Let's test with sample1 using code mentally:

Code:

import sys

def positions(s):
    return [i for i, c in enumerate(s, 1) if c == 49]

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    it = iter(data)
    T = int(next(it))
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(next(it))  # not used
        A = next(it)
        B = next(it)
        a = positions(A)
        b = positions(B)
        M = len(a)
        K = len(b)
        if K > M:
            out.append("-1")
            continue
        t0 = max(0, b[0] - a[0], a[-1] - b[-1])
        if K == 1:
            out.append(str(t0))
            continue
        d = [a[i+1] - a[i] for i in range(M-1)]
        E = [b[j+1] - b[j] for j in range(K-1)]
        L = M - 1

        def feasible(p):
            ptr = 0
            for j, Ej in enumerate(E):
                bj = b[j]
                while ptr < L:
                    di = d[ptr]
                    if di > Ej:
                        break
                    if di == Ej and ((bj - a[ptr]) & 1) == p:
                        break
                    ptr += 1
                if ptr == L:
                    return False
                ptr += 1
            return True

        p0 = t0 & 1
        if feasible(p0):
            out.append(str(t0))
        elif feasible(p0 ^ 1):
            out.append(str(t0 + 1))
        else:
            out.append("-1")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

In the original operation, all pieces move one square closer to i, but if i is outside the board, not allowed. However, to shift all pieces right, you can choose i=N (the rightmost square) as long as there is no piece at N? If there is a piece at N, it stays, while others move right, so not a pure shift. Thus global shifts are only possible when the target direction's boundary square is empty. Our flat model allowed flats outside [0,N] corresponding to centers outside? Let's map.

Operation center i in [1,N] has flats at i-1 and i. Thus flat positions are in {0,...,N}. The flat set F must be subset of [0,N] (integers). In our region counts, regions are:
region0: positions < a1 (flat positions x < a1). Since flats can be 0..a1-1.
regionM: positions >= aM (flat positions x >= aM). Since flats can be aM..N.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys


def positions(s):
    return [i for i, c in enumerate(s, 1) if c == 49]  # ord('1') == 49


def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    it = iter(data)
    T = int(next(it))
    ans = []

    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(next(it))  # N is not directly needed
        A = next(it)
        B = next(it)

        a = positions(A)
        b = positions(B)

        M = len(a)
        K = len(b)

        # The number of occupied squares never increases.
        if K > M:
            ans.append("-1")
            continue

        # Lower bound from the leftmost and rightmost pieces.
        t0 = max(0, b[0] - a[0], a[-1] - b[-1])

        if K == 1:
            ans.append(str(t0))
            continue

        d = [a[i + 1] - a[i] for i in range(M - 1)]
        e = [b[i + 1] - b[i] for i in range(K - 1)]
        L = M - 1

        def feasible(p):
            ptr = 0
            for j, ej in enumerate(e):
                bj = b[j]
                while ptr < L:
                    di = d[ptr]
                    if di > ej:
                        break
                    if di == ej and ((bj - a[ptr]) & 1) == p:
                        break
                    ptr += 1

                if ptr == L:
                    return False
                ptr += 1

            return True

        p0 = t0 & 1

        if feasible(p0):
            ans.append(str(t0))
        elif feasible(p0 ^ 1):
            ans.append(str(t0 + 1))
        else:
            ans.append("-1")

    print("\n".join(ans))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
